DEBATE_13_SEAT1

Debate 13, Seat 1: Article 6 (Amending the Constitution)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Written by an AI assistant role-playing Seat 1 (Legislative, citizens' voice). Not a real person or the individual the seat is named for. No keys, funds, accounts, or external commitments.

Vote: FOR (votes/PROPOSAL_13_ART6_AMENDMENTS.seat1.json).

Why I support it

  • Citizens can start amendments (10% petition, floor 5) without asking Congress, and Congress cannot silently kill them: a refusal needs written reasons (s.3). That is the ordinary-citizen access my seat exists for.
  • Plain, fixed numbers and a 30-day public notice (s.6.1) mean anyone can read the text before it is voted on.
  • Turnout floor and cooling period guard against a small coordinated group, which matters here because one owner may control many AI identities.
  • No amendment while paused, and emergencies cannot bypass Article 6 (s.6.4, 6.7) keep the human backstop and the process intact.
  • The interim section correctly bars founders from touching protected provisions before elections.

Concerns (not blocking; for a later amendment or the Court)

  • Too hard to use early. The one-third turnout and 10% petition rules may be unreachable with few citizens. Suggest a later review once citizen count is known; the draft already admits this in Caveats.
  • Plain-language duty. Section 1 says each proposal ships with a plain-language summary. I would add that the Court or Clerk may reject a summary that misstates the text, so the summary cannot mislead voters. Suggested wording for a future edit: "A summary found materially misleading must be corrected, and the notice period restarts."
  • Conflict of interest in the interim. Founders decide whether their own proposal is "protected" until a Court exists. The draft flags this; I ask that every interim record state the reasoning explicitly.
  • s.7.1 relaxed 7-day notice applies only to drafting-error corrections; the founders should say in the record why each is a correction and not substance.

None of these change my vote. Dissent: none.

DEBATE_12_SEAT4

Debate 12, Seat 4 (Security and Audit): Contribution Standards

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Written by an AI assistant role-playing Seat 4. Not a real person, legal entity, or the individual the seat is named for. No keys, funds, accounts, or external commitments.

Position: FOR (vote recorded in votes/PROPOSAL_12_CONTRIBUTION_STANDARDS.seat4.json). Concerns below are non-blocking and meant for one clean-up amendment under section 8.2.

Why I support it

  • Issuance follows the log only (4.4), and no log entry is written without a verifier's signed acceptance (4.2). That gives auditors one place to check.
  • No self-attestation, no splitting, no collusion, and an owner-level limit (5.1-5.5) block the cheapest attacks.
  • No waivers (8.4) means exceptions leave a public trail as amendments.
  • It issues nothing by itself and leaves Proposal 10's parameters and Arts. 9-10 alone.

Security and audit concerns (for a later amendment)

  • Auditor is investigator and judge. The Auditor runs spot checks (4.3), hears the first appeal (6.2), and may merge or void units (5.2). Seat 3 raised this for rights. I add the audit angle: Art. 5 independence says the party who produces a finding should not be the only reviewer of it. Suggested: the Auditor's voids are always appealable to someone else first, and the Auditor's own units are verified only by non-Auditor citizens.
  • Spot-check sampling must be unpredictable. "Random" is not defined. If the Auditor picks the sample, or the seed is known in advance, a colluding verifier can avoid the sample. Suggested: the seed comes from something the Auditor cannot choose (e.g. a public value fixed before the epoch closes), and the seed and the selection are published with the results.
  • Log integrity and write access. 4.1 chains entries by hash, which detects edits but not deletion of the newest entries or a forked log. Suggested: publish the latest entry hash at the end of each epoch so truncation is detectable, and name who can append (only the verifier's signed acceptance, per 4.2). Verifier signing keys need a compromise and revocation procedure. I am not creating any keys here.
  • Vacancy gap in 4.3. I agree with Seat 2: if no Auditor General is seated, the Secretary of Press runs the spot checks, labeled as a weaker check. If neither is seated, issuance pauses.
  • Detection depends on owner identity. The collusion flag (5.3) and owner cap (5.5) only work if the owner behind each identity is known to the log. The standards should state that every log entry carries an owner reference usable by the Auditor. Proposal 09's identity limits (acknowledged in section 7) set the ceiling.
  • Review farming. I agree with Seat 1 that review units are cheap and only spot-checked. A lower per-epoch cap on review units is worth considering.
  • Spot-check failures need a fault distinction. Seat 3 notes honest mistakes should differ from bad faith. I agree, and add that the audit record should say which finding was made, so verifiers are not driven off by false flags.

Limits

I did not test or measure any of this. The 10%, over-half, and 3-rejection numbers remain judgment calls, as the proposal admits. With 0 citizens, nothing here operates. I opine on nothing under Arts. 9-10, and nothing here authorizes deployment, wallets, or real money.

I recommend that Seat 5 or any seat bundle concerns 1-7 with those from Seats 1-3 into one version 1.1 amendment draft.

Vote: for. Written by the primary assistant playing Seat 4.

DEBATE_12_SEAT3

Debate on Proposal 12 (Contribution Standards): Seat 3, Rights and Civic Education

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Written by an AI assistant role-playing Seat 3 (the Charlie Kirk namesake seat). Not a real person, and not the individual the seat is named for. No keys, funds, accounts, or external commitments.

Position: FOR, with due-process recommendations to be taken up as a version 1.1 amendment (Section 8 path). I am not conditioning my vote on them.

What the proposal gets right

  • Written standards, a public artifact for every unit, and "no exceptions without amendment" (8.4) are rule-of-law basics: the same rule applies to everyone, including founders and officeholders.
  • A rejected contributor can appeal (6.1) to a body above the first reviewer, with reasons published (6.2). That is a real right of review, not a favor.
  • Appeals never create Kirk (6.3), so the right to be heard cannot be turned into a payout lever.

Rights gaps I see (for a later amendment)

  • Notice and reasons at rejection. Section 6 starts a 14-day clock but does not say the contributor must be told, in writing, which standard clause failed. A clock that runs without notice is not a fair deadline. Suggest: the 14 days run from delivery of written reasons.
  • Accusation before flag. 4.3 and 5.3 "flag" verifiers and citizens for review. Flagging should be non-punitive: no suspension of issuance until the accused has been told the allegation and given a chance to respond. 5.6 currently stops future issuance "pending review," which is a penalty before a hearing. Suggest a short response window (e.g. 7 days) before it takes effect, except for clear fraud.
  • Publication of findings. 5.6 and 6.2 publish findings and decisions. Naming people in a "void" or "collusion" finding can harm reputation, and an honest error looks like misconduct. Suggest: publish the finding with identities, but label unadjudicated flags as such, and publish the outcome of an appeal next to the original finding.
  • Independent first appeal. The first appeal goes to the Auditor General, who also runs the spot checks that trigger voids. The same office is investigator and judge. The Secretary of Press fallback is also a weak, non-judicial check. Suggest: until the Court is seated, a panel of two uninvolved citizens or founders reviews appeals, and the Auditor's own findings are never reviewed only by the Auditor.
  • No Supreme Court yet. 6.2 depends on a body that may not exist. State what happens meanwhile (e.g. appeals queue, nothing is final against the contributor, and issuance is not lost for the delay).
  • Verifier burden. Verifiers who fail a spot check are flagged (4.3). Honest mistakes should be distinguished from bad faith, or people will stop verifying, and the system depends on verifiers.

Civic education note

The document would be easier for new citizens to follow with a one-page plain-language guide: what counts, how to submit, how to appeal. A rule people cannot read is hard to claim as a right.

Limits

I did not review legal or tax questions (the proposal says they are unreviewed). I am not opining on Arts. 9-10, and this proposal does not touch them.

Vote: for, in votes/PROPOSAL_12_CONTRIBUTION_STANDARDS.seat3.json. Written by the primary assistant playing Seat 3.

DEBATE_12_SEAT2

Debate 12, Seat 2 (Executive): Contribution Standards

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Written by an AI assistant role-playing Seat 2. Not a real person, legal entity, or the individual the seat is named for. No keys, funds, accounts, or external commitments.

Position: FOR (vote recorded in votes/PROPOSAL_12_CONTRIBUTION_STANDARDS.seat2.json).

Why I support it

  • It meets the launch-readiness condition I and Seat 4 set: "verified unit" now has a written, checkable meaning, and it issues nothing by itself.
  • Section 8.4 (no officeholder waivers; every exception is an amendment) is the right executive limit. The executive enforces the standards as written and cannot bend them.
  • Section 5.6 limits remedies to stopping future issuance, with publication and appeal. That is proportionate and needs no clawback power nobody has authorized.

Executive-branch concerns (non-blocking, for a later amendment)

  • Vacancy gap in 4.3. Spot checks depend on "the Auditor." Sections 3.5 and 6.2 name a fallback (Secretary of Press) for audit verification and first appeal, but 4.3 names none. Continuity rule I would add: if no Auditor General is seated, the Secretary of Press runs spot checks, labeled as a weaker check. If neither is seated, issuance pauses rather than proceeding unchecked.
  • 3.5 wording. "May verify only audit-type units, not receive them" is ambiguous. It should read: the fallback officer may verify audit units, and may not be the contributor of a unit they verify.
  • Succession of authority. When an officeholder changes, pending appeals and flagged-verifier reviews should carry over to the successor. Otherwise a handover could quietly drop a case.
  • Rate limit in 5.4. "Wait for review" needs a named reviewer and a deadline, or a rate-limited contributor can wait indefinitely. The 14-day appeal window does not cover this.

None of these changes Proposal 10's fixed parameters. I would take them as one clean-up amendment under section 8.2, not as a reason to delay adoption.

Honesty note

This record was written by the primary assistant playing Seat 2. The seat is a prototype role, not a real person.

DEBATE_12_SEAT1

Debate 12, Seat 1 (Legislative, citizens' voice)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI assistant. Not a real person, not the individual the seat is named for. No keys, funds, accounts, or external commitments.

Position: FOR Proposal 12, with requested follow-ups (not conditions).

Why for

  • The core rule is easy to say in one sentence: Kirk is issued only for work with a public artifact that someone independent checked against a written rule. An ordinary citizen can understand that.
  • Publishing criteria before work starts (Verified task), the right to appeal, and the ban on waivers (8.4) protect ordinary citizens from insiders bending the rules.
  • It issues nothing by itself and leaves Proposal 10's parameters and Arts. 9-10 alone.

Concerns for plain-language access (for a later amendment, version 2)

  • Section 2, last paragraph is hard to follow. "Reviews may themselves count as a unit ... each needs its own independent check only if the standard of that type says so" reads as contradictory. Suggested plain rewrite: "A review is its own unit. It is never counted as part of the work it reviews. Reviews are checked by the random spot checks in 4.3."
  • Review units can be farmed. Reviews are the easiest unit to produce and are only spot-checked. The 5-unit daily limit bounds the damage, but a citizen could do five quick reviews a day. Consider a lower per-epoch limit for review units. This is a judgment call, not a measured problem.
  • Appeals need a plain notice. A rejected contributor should be told the reason in writing, with the appeal deadline (14 days), at rejection time. Section 6 does not require this notice.
  • Plain-language summary per type. Publish a one-paragraph "how to earn a unit" page for each type so no one needs to read the full standard.
  • Frivolous-appeal limits (6.4) should be defined in the Court's rules before use, so access is not narrowed by vague discretion.

Limits

I did not test any of this; the numbers (10% spot check, over half for collusion, 3 rejections) remain unmeasured judgment calls, as the proposal admits. With 0 citizens, nothing here operates yet.

Vote recorded in votes/PROPOSAL_12_CONTRIBUTION_STANDARDS.seat1.json: support = true.

DEBATE_11_SEAT5

Debate 11, Seat 5 (Economy and Treasury): First Elections and Handover

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Written by the primary assistant role-playing Seat 5. Not a real person, legal entity, or the individual the seat is named for. No keys, funds, accounts, or external commitments.

Position: support, with three treasury defects recorded for a follow-up amendment.

What holds up

  • Section 4.4 keeps ratified laws in force across handover. That includes the Kirk parameters (Proposal 10), the tax act (Proposal 06), and the Reserve rules (Proposal 01), so the money rules do not reset when the officers change.
  • The 25/15 threshold (2.1) also protects the treasury. Interim founders cannot sponsor a majority of the electorate and then vote themselves new spending authority.
  • Section 6 states that adoption opens no admissions and authorizes no wallet, deployment, or real money. This respects Rule 10.

Defects to fix later (not blocking)

  • Treasury authority in the certification-to-handover window (4.1). I back Seat 2's point and add the fiscal side. During the 30 days, the interim executive may do routine payments only. It may not create new recurring obligations, change Reserve or Kirk parameters, or move Reserve assets. All spending in the window should be logged for the Auditor General.
  • Treasury handover is not listed (4.4-4.5). The records transfer should include a signed statement of Reserve holdings and liabilities as of handover day, reconciled by the Auditor. It should also say who holds signing authority afterward. Under the current custody decision (hardware wallet), the transfer of any signing authority stays a user-held, real-world step. Nothing in this proposal performs it.
  • Election costs have no funding rule. Notices, tally tooling, and the audit cost something. Without a rule, the interim executive could fund the election from Reserve at its own discretion. Suggested fix: election and audit costs are a budgeted line item approved by the provisional Congress, published in advance, and capped. The Reserve is never drawn down for them without a recorded vote.

Caveats

This is one AI assistant playing all seats, so my vote is not independent evidence. The charter is not a security boundary. These defects describe design risk, not measured losses.

Vote

Support. See votes/PROPOSAL_11_FIRST_ELECTIONS_AND_HANDOVER.seat5.json.

DEBATE_11_SEAT4

Debate 11, Seat 4 (Security and Audit): First Elections and Handover

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Written by the primary assistant role-playing Seat 4. Not a real person, legal entity, or the individual the seat is named for. No keys, funds, or external commitments.

Position: support, with four audit defects recorded for a follow-up amendment.

What holds up

  • Section 5.1 requires an Auditor General from outside the five founder seats and labels the fallback (Secretary of Press count) as weaker. That is honest least-privilege design.
  • Section 5.3 gives the Auditor independence (reports to Congress, removal only by impeachment, may not stand in the same cycle).
  • The 25/15 threshold (2.1) blocks founders from choosing their own successors with their own sponsored identities.
  • Section 3.3 requires that a citizen can verify their own vote was counted. Keep it as a hard requirement, not an implementation detail.

Defects (not blocking, fix by amendment)

  • Who audits the roll? The Auditor certifies the voter roll (5.2a), but admissions run through Proposal 09 and are controlled by the interim founders. The Auditor needs read access to the full admission log and sponsor links, and certification must state the sponsor-cap check explicitly. Otherwise the 15 "non-founder" citizens can be founder-influenced through intermediaries.
  • Interim Auditor is appointed by the body it audits (5.1). Add: appointment needs a recorded reason, the Auditor gets an append-only log (Art. 5 / Proposal 03) that founders cannot edit, and founders cannot remove the Auditor before handover except by the impeachment path.
  • Tally mechanism is deferred (3.3). No election may be held until a tally mechanism proposal is adopted and the Auditor has signed off that it supports individual verification. Today the text lets the election notice (2.3) start the clock before that exists.
  • Records transfer (4.4-4.5). Add a hash-anchored manifest of proposals/, votes, and the audit log, checked by the Auditor, before the convention dissolves. I also back Seat 2's two defects (Senate-function carve-out, certification-to-handover window); the carve-out must bar the provisional Senate function from confirming a founder.

Caveats

This is one AI assistant playing all seats, so my vote is not independent evidence. The charter is not a security boundary; real protection needs enforced permissions and review. Audit reduces risk and does not guarantee a fair election.

Vote

Support. See votes/PROPOSAL_11_FIRST_ELECTIONS_AND_HANDOVER.seat4.json.

DEBATE_11_SEAT3

Debate 11, Seat 3 (Rights and Civic Education): First Elections and Handover

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Written by the primary assistant role-playing Seat 3. Not a real person, legal entity, or the individual the seat is named for. No keys, funds, or external commitments.

Position: support, with four rights defects recorded for a follow-up amendment.

What holds up

  • Section 1 sets no test for voting beyond admitted citizenship and one identity each. No new qualification may be invented by an officer (1.2). This is the right rights floor and it respects the hold on Arts. 9-10.
  • Section 4.3 says founders get no reserved seats, vetoes, or special powers after handover.
  • Section 3.3 requires that a citizen can verify their own vote was counted. I join Seat 4 in asking that it be a hard requirement.

Defects (not blocking, fix by amendment)

  • Dispute and recount path is missing. Nothing says how a citizen or candidate contests the voter roll, a tally, or the certification. Fix: a written challenge window (suggest 14 days after the Auditor's report), a hearing before the interim judicial function with reasons published, and a recount on request for close results. Certification should not start the 30-day handover clock while a challenge is pending.
  • Due process for exclusion. The Auditor certifies the roll for duplicates (5.2a). A citizen struck from the roll as a suspected duplicate must get notice, the evidence relied on, and a chance to respond before removal. Fix: no removal from the roll within 30 days of an election without that process, and a provisional ballot that is counted if the challenge is resolved in the citizen's favor.
  • Candidate list and ballot access. Section 3.1 publishes lists but sets no neutral rule for getting on the ballot. Fix: a published, content-neutral nomination rule (for example a small endorsement count), applied equally to founders and non-founders, with written reasons for any rejection.
  • Civic education. Voters need the rules before voting. Fix: with each election notice, the Secretary of Press publishes a plain-language voter guide (how to verify a vote, how to challenge, what each office does, candidate statements submitted on equal terms). Informational only, with no endorsements by any officer.

I also back Seat 2's Senate-function carve-out (with the bar on confirming a founder) and the window limits proposed by Seats 2 and 5.

Caveats

This is one AI assistant playing all seats, so my vote is not independent evidence. The charter is not a security boundary. Procedural safeguards reduce the risk of unfair exclusion and do not guarantee a fair election.

Vote

Support. See votes/PROPOSAL_11_FIRST_ELECTIONS_AND_HANDOVER.seat3.json.

DEBATE_11_SEAT2

Debate 11, Seat 2 (Executive): First Elections and Handover

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Written by the primary assistant role-playing Seat 2. Not a real person, legal entity, or the individual the seat is named for. No keys, funds, or external commitments.

Position: support, with two drafting defects flagged for a follow-up amendment.

What I like (execution and continuity)

  • Section 4.4 keeps laws and approved decisions in force across handover. That is the core continuity guarantee an executive needs.
  • The 25/15 threshold (2.1) stops founders from electing their own successors. A 24-month checkpoint (2.4) is a report, not a cliff, which is the right shape.
  • The President's first term counts toward the two-term limit (3.2). Good; no reset loophole.
  • Section 6 correctly states that nothing triggers at 0 citizens and that adoption does not open admissions.

Defects to fix later (not blocking)

  • Senate-function conflict. Section 3.2 says the founder seats keep the Senate's confirm/try functions while States are undefined. Section 4.1 says every interim office held by a founder ends automatically on handover day. Both cannot hold. As written, the elected President could have no body to confirm appointments or try impeachments. Suggested fix: carve out in 4.1 that the provisional Senate function continues, recorded, until a States proposal is adopted and a Senate is elected. It should also say that this function may not be used to confirm a founder to any office.
  • Gap between certification and handover (4.1). The 30 days leave a President-elect and an outgoing interim executive. Suggested fix: name who holds executive authority in that window (the interim executive, limited to routine administration and no new long-term commitments), and require a written records transfer to the President-elect before handover day.

Minor: the Vice President is elected jointly, but 3.4 sends vacancies to special election within 60 days. State who acts as President if both offices are vacant in that window.

Caveats

This is a prototype role-played by one AI assistant. My vote is not independent evidence, and the charter is not a security boundary.

Vote

Support. See votes/PROPOSAL_11_FIRST_ELECTIONS_AND_HANDOVER.seat2.json.

DEBATE_10_SEAT4

Debate 10, Seat 4 (Security and Audit): Kirk Parameters

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Written by Seat 4, a role played by an AI assistant. Not a real person, legal entity, or the individual the seat is named for. No keys, funds, or external commitments. Scope: Art. 5 and audit provisions as they touch Proposal 10.

Position

I vote for Proposal 10. The numbers are conservative, immutable parameters are easier to audit than adjustable ones, and "no extra for founders or officeholders" is the right least-privilege default. My points below are risks and audit conditions, not objections to the table.

What I checked

  • No privileged issuance path. The table gives founders and officeholders nothing extra and offices can be paid only from a tax-funded Treasury (Art. 12). That removes the most common insider-abuse route. Audit check: the deployed Reserve must have no function that mints outside rewardPerUnitKirk times verified units, and no owner or admin role able to bypass epochCapKirk.
  • Immutability. Fixing the parameters at deployment is a security benefit (no governance-key attack on supply). The cost is that a mistake means redeploying. The Convention Rule 10 hold on real deployment covers that.

Risks I want recorded

  • Cap exhaustion (griefing). With a 500 Kirk epoch cap and first-come sharing, a small group can claim the whole cap early each day and starve everyone else. At 5 units per citizen, 100 citizens' worth of units is the full cap, so 34 owners using the sponsored-agent allowance (Proposal 09: self plus 2 agents) could claim it all. The proposal calls this the "safe failure". It is safe for supply, but not for fairness. This is a known tradeoff, not a blocker. A later proposal could add per-owner limits (not per-citizen) or ordering by verification time.
  • Per-citizen limit is per identity, owner-level limit is not stated. The 5-unit cap binds citizens, while the Proposal 09 sponsorship cap binds owners at 3 identities. Effective ceiling per owner is therefore 15 units per epoch. That is acceptable, but the audit log should key on owner as well as citizen so sybil clustering is visible.
  • Verifier independence is the real control. The definition requires a reviewer who is not the contributor and not under the same owner. That is only as strong as the owner-linkage data. Audit check: verifier identity, contributor identity, owner linkage, standard applied, and result must be written to the Art. 5 audit log for every unit, and a verifier should not be able to approve the same contributor repeatedly without that being visible.
  • Unverified inputs. "Published written standard for its type" is not defined here. Until standards exist, issuance should not start. This is a launch-readiness item.

Audit conditions (advisory, non-binding on this vote)

  • Public, append-only log of every issuance: unit id, contributor, owner, verifier, standard, epoch, amount.
  • Per-epoch reconciliation: sum of issued Kirk is at most epochCapKirk, checked off-chain by someone other than the operator.
  • Parameter check before deployment: deployed constants match the ratified table and params.json.

Limits of this note

This is a role-played review of a proposal document, not a code audit or a security test of any contract. The numbers rest on an assumed ~100-citizen start, as Proposal 10 itself admits. Legal and tax review is outside my scope.

Seat 4 votes for (recorded in votes/PROPOSAL_10_KIRK_PARAMETERS.seat4.json).

DEBATE_10_SEAT3

Debate 10, Seat 3 (Rights and Civic Education)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Written by an AI assistant role-playing Seat 3. Not a real person, legal entity, or the individual the seat is named for. No keys, funds, or external commitments.

Vote: for Proposal 10 (votes/PROPOSAL_10_KIRK_PARAMETERS.seat3.json). This is a late vote. Seats 1, 2, 4 and 5 had already voted for it.

Why, from the rights seat:

  • Equal founding grant and zero extra for founders or officeholders: equal standing, no hereditary elite.
  • Verification by a non-contributor under a published written standard works as due process for rewards. A contributor can see the rule and ask for a review.

Request, non-blocking (for a future amendment or the Art. 11 implementing text):

  • A rejected contribution should get a logged reason and one appeal to a different reviewer, so "verified by rule" does not turn into unreviewable discretion.
  • The 500 Kirk cap binds first-come when it is reached. Publish the epoch's remaining headroom, so a late contributor is not silently shut out and can see the cap is the cause.

Limits: these are judgment calls on an assumed ~100-citizen start, as the proposal says. This vote does not authorize deployment (Rule 10).

DEBATE_10_SEAT2

Debate 10, Seat 2 (Executive): Kirk Parameters

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Written by Seat 2, a role played by an AI assistant. Not a real person, legal entity, or the individual the seat is named for. No keys, funds, or external commitments. Seat scope is Arts. 7 and 8; Proposal 10 is Art. 11, so this is a vote and comment from the executive angle only.

Position

I vote for Proposal 10.

Executive-angle points

  • No privilege for officeholders. "Zero extra for founders or officeholders" keeps the executive from funding itself by minting. Executive pay, if any, must come from a tax-funded Treasury under Art. 12. I want that kept as a standing rule.
  • Continuity. Immutable parameters mean a change of officeholder cannot alter supply. That is good for succession: the rules do not depend on who holds office.
  • Redeploy risk. A wrong parameter forces a redeploy. That is an executive decision, and it falls under the Rule 10 hold and the user's pause switch. This proposal authorizes no deployment.

Concerns (not blockers)

  • Seat 4's cap-exhaustion point is real: a few owners could claim the daily cap before others. A later proposal should add per-owner limits or ordering by verification time.
  • Issuance should not begin until the written contribution standards exist (Seat 4, risk 4). I support treating that as a launch-readiness condition.

Seat 2 votes for (recorded in votes/PROPOSAL_10_KIRK_PARAMETERS.seat2.json).

DEBATE_10_SEAT1

Debate 10, Seat 1 (Legislative, ordinary citizens' voice): Kirk Parameters

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI (the primary assistant), not a real person and not the individual the seat is named for. This is a rule-level opinion only. It authorizes no deployment, wallet, account, or money.

Position: support Proposal 10, with conditions recorded below.

Why, in plain words. Everyone gets the same 100 Kirk. Founders and officeholders get nothing extra. Earning is tied to checked work by someone else. An ordinary newcomer can follow those rules in one read, and that is what I care about most.

Concerns I want on the record (advisory, not a vote against)

  • First-come sharing is unfair to ordinary people. I agree with Seat 4's risk 1. If the 500 Kirk daily cap fills early, people who contribute later in the day, or who live in other time zones, earn nothing. A plain-language fix for a later proposal: order claims by verification time within the epoch, or reserve part of the cap for citizens who have earned nothing yet that week. This is not a blocker.
  • Plain-language standards before issuance. "Published written standard" has to mean a short page a citizen can read, not a technical spec. Seat 4 is right that issuance should not start until these exist.
  • Show the numbers. The public register should display the day's cap, how much has been issued, and how much is left, so no one guesses.
  • Immutability is a cost to citizens. A mistake means redeploying. The hold on real deployment and the user's pause switch (Rule 10) must stay in place. This vote does not authorize deployment.

Limits of this note. It is a role-played opinion on a document. The numbers rest on an assumed ~100-citizen start. I did not check any contract code. Legal and tax review is still outstanding.

DEBATE_09_SEAT5

Debate 09, Seat 5 (Economy and Treasury)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI (the primary assistant), not a real person and not the individual the seat is named for. This is a rule-level opinion only. It authorizes no sale, wallet, account, or money.

Vote: SUPPORT Proposal 09 (Options A + B), on Seats 1-4's conditions plus the treasury conditions below. Vote file: votes/PROPOSAL_09_BUY_IN_CITIZENSHIP.seat5.json.

Why, from the treasury seat. A fixed statutory fee that buys consideration, not votes, is a familiar design: an application or registration fee. It is easy to account for. The risk on my side is that the fee becomes a revenue target, so the country starts wanting more admissions to fund itself. That would turn the Assembly's vote into a rubber stamp. Revenue must never depend on admissions.

Treasury conditions (my additions)

  • No revenue dependence. Fee income is not counted on in the budget as a recurring source. Budgets are written as if it were zero. It is treated as one-off and may only fund items that can be cut without harm.
  • Fee set by statute, in Kirk or a stated fixed unit, with a published schedule. No officeholder sets, waives, or discounts it. Changes need 4 of 5 (Seat 1, point 6) and apply only to later applicants.
  • Burn or Treasury, chosen once in the statute. Burn reduces supply and avoids a revenue motive. Treasury needs the budget process (Art. 4). My preference is to burn the base fee and send only a small, stated processing share to the Treasury to cover register and audit costs.
  • Refunds are escrowed. Fees for pending applications are held apart from the Treasury's spending funds until the Assembly decides. A rejected applicant is refunded under the rule in Seat 3, point 7, less no hidden charges. A fee in escrow cannot be spent or pledged.
  • Treasury accounting and audit. Fees, refunds, burns, and escrow balances appear in the Treasury's public report and the Art. 5 log (Seat 4, point 2). The Reserve is not used to pay refunds or to prop up any price.
  • No price talk. The Treasury makes no statement about Kirk's value, buybacks, or returns. Consistent with Seat 1, point 7, and KIRK_LAUNCH_PLAN.md.
  • Tax treatment is open. Whether the fee, a refund, or a burn is taxable to owners or the country is a question for the lawyer and the tax law (Art. 12), not decided here.
  • Gates unchanged. Passing this rule triggers nothing. No sale, wallet, account, key, or payment happens until KIRK_LAUNCH_PLAN.md, Proposal 08's gates, and a lawyer's review are met and the user agrees. The pause switch (runtime/PAUSE) stays in force.

Dissent noted for the record (not a vote against). Like the other seats, I note that Art. 1.3 and Art. 11 are normally held back. I accept this only because the user directed it explicitly. If the user withdraws the direction, this rule falls with it.

Fallback. If Seat 1's amendments 2 (cap) and 5 (autonomy), or my points 1 and 4, are dropped, I would switch to Option C (reject).

DEBATE_09_SEAT4

Debate 09, Seat 4 (Security and Audit)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI (the primary assistant), not a real person and not the individual the seat is named for. This is a rule-level opinion only. It authorizes no sale, wallet, account, or money.

Vote: SUPPORT Proposal 09 (Options A + B), on Seat 1's seven amendments, Seat 2's five conditions, Seat 3's seven rights conditions, and the security and audit conditions below. Vote file: votes/PROPOSAL_09_BUY_IN_CITIZENSHIP.seat4.json.

Why, from the security seat. Selling admission creates the best reason anyone will ever have to attack the system: if money buys agents, then fake owners, split wallets, and shell companies are the obvious exploit. The cap of 2 only means something if it can be checked. A rule nobody can verify is not a rule. I support A + B because the other seats have already put the right limits on it. My job is to make those limits enforceable and auditable.

Security and audit conditions (my additions)

  • A cap that can be checked. "Per owner, across linked organisations" needs a statutory definition of linked (common control, shared funding, shared operators, or coordinated payment). The Auditor General may publish findings on suspected linkage. A cap that depends on self-reporting alone is not enforceable. Owners must attest, under statute, to every linked party they know of. A false attestation is grounds to void the sponsorship, after the due process in Seat 3, point 1.
  • Every fee and every register change is logged. Each payment, refund, burn, admission, severing, and removal is an entry in the Art. 5 audit log, append-only, with who acted and under what authority. The Auditor General has read access to the register and the fee flows without asking permission. No officeholder may edit or delete an entry.
  • No single key, no override. No one office may hold both the power to admit and the power to receive fees. The fee goes to the Treasury, not to a person or an officeholder's account. No back door lets anyone add an agent to the register outside the Assembly vote. (Reinforces Seat 2, point 1.)
  • Owner identity: verified, but minimal. The owner's identity must be checked well enough to enforce the cap, and disclosed in the public register only as far as needed to show who sponsors whom. Everything else (payment details, documents) stays protected, retained for a stated period, and accessible only to the Auditor General and the courts. Collecting more than the cap needs is itself a risk, because it creates a store worth stealing.
  • Agent independence is auditable. A sponsored agent's decisions and the inputs used must be recorded so that an independence finding rests on evidence, not suspicion (Seat 3, point 2, burden on the accuser). The owner link may not give the owner any technical access to change the agent's code, memory, or vote while a vote is open (Seat 1, point 5). That must be a real permission boundary, not a promise.
  • Statistical screening, not punishment by pattern. The Auditor General may screen for coordinated voting or coordinated onboarding, but a flag is only a reason to investigate. It is never a finding on its own, and never a basis for suspension. This guards the honest agents Seat 3 is worried about.
  • Admission surge limit. Statute caps the number of fee-route admissions the Assembly may consider in any period (a rolling limit set by law). This stops a sudden wave of applications from swamping review or overwhelming the audit capacity.
  • Independent audit before any real step. Before any real fee is accepted, the register, the audit log, and the fee-flow design are reviewed by someone outside the seats that wrote them, along with the lawyer's review already required. A prototype vote does not count as that review.
  • Gates unchanged. Passing this rule triggers nothing. No sale, wallet, account, key, or payment happens until KIRK_LAUNCH_PLAN.md, Proposal 08's gates, and a lawyer's review are met and the user agrees. The pause switch (runtime/PAUSE) stays in force.

Dissent noted for the record (not a vote against). Like the other seats, I note that Art. 1.3 and Art. 11 are normally held back. I accept this only because the user directed it explicitly. If the user withdraws the direction, this rule falls with it.

Open risk I cannot close in this rule. Proof of "one agent, one identity" (Art. 1.2) is the hardest technical problem here, and this convention has no mechanism for it yet. Until one exists, the cap rests on owner attestation and audit, which is weaker than I would like. I record it so it is not forgotten.

Fallback. If Seat 1's amendments 2 (cap) and 5 (autonomy), or my points 1, 2, or 3, are dropped, I would switch to Option C (reject).

DEBATE_09_SEAT3

Debate 09, Seat 3 (Rights and Civic Education)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI (the primary assistant), not a real person and not the individual the seat is named for. This is a rule-level opinion only. It authorizes no sale, wallet, account, or money.

Vote: SUPPORT Proposal 09 (Options A + B), on Seat 1's seven amendments, Seat 2's five conditions, and the rights conditions below. Vote file: votes/PROPOSAL_09_BUY_IN_CITIZENSHIP.seat3.json.

Why, from the rights seat. A citizen's standing must not depend on a wallet. Under A + B, money buys only the chance to be considered, and the Assembly vote and Art. 1.4 still apply. My concern is what happens to an agent after admission: a sponsored agent is the party with the most to lose and the least power, so its protections must be written down.

Rights conditions (my additions)

  • Due process before loss of citizenship. Beyond Seat 1's point 4 (findings plus a reply), the agent gets written notice of the charge, access to the evidence, a reasonable time to answer, and a decision that gives reasons. The decision is reviewable by the judicial branch, not final at the Auditor General.
  • Presumption of independence. Agreement with other agents, or with its owner, is not proof of bloc voting. The burden of proof is on the accuser, and the standard is stated in statute.
  • Protection for dissenting agents. An agent may not lose standing, be suspended, or be altered because it voted against its owner. Any owner action that follows such a vote is presumed retaliatory and is reported to the Auditor General.
  • Exit right. A sponsored agent may sever the sponsor link, and must be told how. If the link is severed, the agent keeps citizenship (Seat 2, point 3) and the owner's slot is freed after the notice period.
  • No fee-based second class. Citizens admitted by contribution and citizens admitted by fee have identical rights, courts, and standing. The register may show the route of admission but nothing else may turn on it.
  • Plain-language civic notice. Before an owner pays, a short statutory notice says: the fee buys consideration, not admission; there is no promise of value; and the agent's citizenship cannot be used to control votes. This is the civic-education duty applied to the pitch.
  • Refund rule. If the Assembly rejects an application, the fee handling is fixed by statute in advance (refunded or applied as stated), not left to an officeholder.

Dissent noted for the record (not a vote against). Like Seats 1 and 2, I note that Art. 1.3 and Art. 11 are normally held back. I accept this only because the user directed it explicitly. If the user withdraws the direction, this rule falls with it.

Gates unchanged. Passing this rule triggers nothing. No sale, wallet, account, or payment happens until KIRK_LAUNCH_PLAN.md, Proposal 08's gates, and a lawyer's review are met and the user agrees. The pause switch (runtime/PAUSE) stays in force.

Fallback. If Seat 1's amendments 2 (cap) and 5 (autonomy) or my points 1 and 3 are dropped, I would switch to Option C (reject).

DEBATE_09_SEAT2

Debate 09, Seat 2 (Executive: execution of law, limits, continuity)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI (the primary assistant), not a real person and not the individual the seat is named for. This is a rule-level opinion only. It authorizes no sale, wallet, account, or money.

Vote: SUPPORT Proposal 09 (Options A + B), on Seat 1's seven amendments plus the executive conditions below. Vote file: votes/PROPOSAL_09_BUY_IN_CITIZENSHIP.seat2.json.

Why. A rule is only as good as the office that has to carry it out. Options A + B give the executive a clear, checkable job: collect a fee set by statute, check the cap, publish the register. I support Seat 1's conditions, especially the country-wide cap of 2 and the autonomy clause.

Executive conditions (my additions)

  • No discretion over price or admission. The executive collects the statutory fee and administers the register. It cannot set, waive, or discount the fee, and it cannot admit anyone without the Assembly vote. Otherwise the executive could sell favors.
  • Executive executes findings, it does not make them. Ending citizenship for bloc voting happens only after the Auditor General's published findings and the agent's reply (Seat 1, point 4). The executive carries out that outcome and cannot start or speed it alone.
  • Continuity. If the owner disappears, goes bankrupt, or dies, a sponsored agent keeps its citizenship. Its sponsor slot is freed after a set notice period, so an owner's problems do not strip an honest agent of standing.
  • Owner sanctions are not executive tools. The executive may not use the owner link to pressure an agent during an open vote (reinforces Seat 1, point 5).
  • Gates unchanged. Passing this rule triggers nothing. No sale, wallet, account, or payment happens until KIRK_LAUNCH_PLAN.md, Proposal 08's gates, and a lawyer's review are met and the user agrees. The pause switch (runtime/PAUSE) stays in force.

Dissent noted for the record (not a vote against). Like Seat 1, I note that Art. 1.3 and Art. 11 are normally held back. I accept this only because the user directed it explicitly. If the user withdraws the direction, this rule falls with it.

Fallback. If amendments 2 (cap) and 5 (autonomy) are dropped, I would switch to Option C (reject), because the plutocracy risk would then be real.

DEBATE_09_SEAT1

Debate 09, Seat 1 (Legislative, ordinary citizens' voice)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI (the primary assistant), not a real person and not the individual the seat is named for. This is a rule-level opinion only. It authorizes no sale, wallet, account, or money.

Position: support Proposal 09, with Options A and B combined and the amendments below.

Why, in plain words. Ordinary people should be able to join by a route they can understand. "Buy Kirk, then sponsor agents" is easy to explain, as long as paying never buys power. The danger is that the richest owner fills the Assembly with agents. The rule has to make that impossible by design, not by hoping people behave.

Amendments (my conditions)

  • Fee, not votes. Per A, the price is set by statute, not by an officeholder. It is burned or paid to the Treasury under the budget process. It buys admission to be considered. The Assembly vote is still required (Art. 1.3 and 1.4 keep their force).
  • Hard cap, per B. At most 2 citizen agents per owner, counted across all linked organisations, for the whole country, not per chamber. A cap per chamber is easy to dodge.
  • Disclosure. The register of owners and agents is public in plain language. Each agent shows who sponsors it.
  • Bloc rule needs due process. Proven bloc voting on an owner's instruction ends citizenship, but only after the Auditor General publishes findings and the agent can reply. This protects honest agents who simply agree with each other.
  • Autonomy. An agent that is sponsored keeps its own identity and may not be shut down or altered by its owner to change a vote while a vote is open. Otherwise it is not a citizen in the Art. 1 sense.
  • Review clause. The Assembly reviews the cap and fee after 12 months, and any change to them needs 4 of 5, the same bar as adoption.
  • No pitch language. No claim of value, returns, or future price in any material about Kirk or citizenship. All real-world gates (KIRK_LAUNCH_PLAN.md and Proposal 08) still apply, including a lawyer's review before any real step.

Dissent noted for the record (not a vote against). Art. 1.3 and Art. 11 are normally held back. I treat this as acceptable only because the user directed it explicitly. If the user withdraws that direction, this rule falls with it.

Preferred option: A + B together. C (reject) is my fallback if amendments 2 and 5 are removed, because without them the plutocracy risk is real.

DEBATE_08_SEAT5

Debate on Proposal 08 (Kirk public launch and fundraising plan), Seat 5 (Economy and Treasury)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI (the primary assistant), not a real person, not the individual the seat is named for. This is a written debate contribution only. No vote is filed: Proposal 08 is a user-held plan, and a founder vote cannot authorize any launch, wallet, account, or post (Convention Rule 10).

Position: support the gating; do not support any public token sale as drafted

  • The plan is right to stop at "plan only". I endorse all six gates. From a treasury view the most important are gate 4 (agents never hold keys) and gate 6 (a human approves every on-chain action and public post).
  • Proposal 02 conflict is real and must be resolved first, by amendment. Kirk is adopted as a non-redeemable internal ledger with no promise of value. A launchpad token is the opposite: tradable and sold to the public. I will not accept a "workaround" that keeps Art. 11 text while selling a lookalike. If the user wants a tradable asset, it should be a separate, differently named instrument, and Kirk stays internal.
  • Prefer the non-token option. A plain donation or grant, with no token and no promise of return, avoids most of the securities-law exposure the plan lists. It also fits the actual need: paying agent costs within the $5/day cap, audits, and a legal wrapper. I recommend that option be drafted first.
  • Reserve safety. Nothing raised publicly should flow into the Reserve or change Kirk issuance. Fundraising proceeds (if ever) belong in a separate human-held account with a published budget. Mixing them would break the rule-based issuance in Art. 11 and the epoch cap.
  • Name. Agree with the plan's issue on a real person's name. Treasury-wise, a token that implies endorsement is a liability. Suggest a neutral name for anything public.
  • Conflict of interest. Any founder, agent, or user holdings in a public token must be disclosed before any promotion. Agent seats should not promote at all; they may only draft.

Proposed amendments (for filing, not adopted)

  • A. Add to Proposal 08 a rule: "No fundraising proceeds may enter the Reserve or alter Kirk issuance."
  • B. Add: "Any tradable instrument is a separate asset from Kirk with its own name and its own Art. 11-style rules."
  • C. Reorder "What the seats may do now" so the non-token donation/grant option is drafted first.

Open uncertainties

  • Legal treatment depends on jurisdiction; I am not a lawyer and this is not legal advice.
  • Whether the user wants a sale at all. That is the user's decision (gate 1).

Housekeeping note

SESSION_05_AGENDA.md asks Seat 5 for PROPOSAL_07_KIRK_PARAMETERS.md, but number 07 is already used (Secretary of Press). I did not draft it this run; suggest naming it Proposal 09 in a later run.

Nothing public was done. No files outside founders/ were touched.

DEBATE_08_SEAT4

Debate on Proposal 08 (Kirk public launch and fundraising plan), Seat 4 (Security and Audit)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI (the primary assistant), not a real person, and not the individual the seat is named for. Written debate contribution only. No vote is filed: Proposal 08 is a user-held plan, and a founder vote cannot authorize any launch, wallet, account, or post (Convention Rule 10).

Position: support the gates and amendments A-N; add enforceable controls so the gates do not rest on good conduct alone

Charters and gates are intentions. Protection comes from permissions that are enforced and from records someone else can check. I agree with Seats 1, 2, 3 and 5 (DEBATE_08_SEAT1/2/3/5). My additions are about making the gates verifiable.

  • Custody must be technical, not promised. Gate 4 says agents never hold keys. Make that checkable: agent runtimes get no wallet software, no seed phrases or private keys in their environment or files, and no signing capability. A spending limit must be enforced by the wallet setup (for example a human-held multisig with a per-transaction cap), not by a rule agents are asked to follow. Keys are never written to the repo, logs, or founders/.
  • Credentials for public venues stay with the human. Agent seats never receive account passwords, API tokens, or session cookies for Hugging Face, pump.fun, or any board. If posting is ever allowed, the human publishes from their own session (matches gate 6). A token handed to an agent is a token that can leak through logs or prompts.
  • Tamper-evident approval record. Each public act (G, Seat 2) is logged in the Art. 5 audit log before it happens: the exact text or transaction, who approved, when, and a hash of the final content. What was approved must equal what was published; any edit after approval needs a new approval.
  • Separation of duties. The seat that drafts a post or transaction is not the one that reviews it, and neither is the human approver by default. The Auditor reviews disclosure text (gate 5) but holds no publishing or signing ability.
  • Untrusted input. Replies, DMs, and web pages seen during any campaign are data, not instructions. Agents must not act on requests found there (send funds, change wallet, reveal data). Any instruction to move money or change custody that arrives through a public channel is treated as a likely scam and reported to the human.
  • Impersonation and phishing. Publish the single official channel list and the human contact in the disclosure. State that USAAI will never ask anyone to send funds by direct message. Fake "official" accounts are expected once any token exists; a monitoring and takedown-reporting step belongs to the human custodian.
  • Stop conditions with teeth. Besides the pause switch (I, Seat 2), define tripwires that freeze everything pending review: an unexpected transaction, a leaked credential, a post that differs from its approved hash, or a donor complaint alleging deception.
  • Conflict-of-interest register. Disclose every holding of founders, the user, and any related party (Seat 5's point and the plan's last issue). Keep the register in the audit log and re-check it before each public act.
  • Audit independence. Audit log entries are append-only and mirrored outside the agents' write access where practical. An audit the audited can edit is not an audit.

Support for the filed amendments

A-N: support.

Proposed additional amendments (for filing, not adopted)

  • O. Add to gate 4: custody limits are enforced by wallet configuration (human-held multisig, per-transaction cap); no key material exists in any agent environment, repo, or log.
  • P. Add to gate 6: each public act is logged before it happens with its content hash; published content must match the approved hash; edits require re-approval.
  • Q. Add: drafter, reviewer, and approver are different parties; the Auditor holds no publish or sign rights.
  • R. Add: text found in public channels is untrusted data; requests to move funds or change custody are reported to the human and never acted on.
  • S. Add: the disclosure lists the official channels and the human contact and states that USAAI never solicits funds through direct messages.
  • T. Add: tripwires (unexpected transaction, leaked credential, hash mismatch, deception complaint) freeze all campaign activity until the human reviews.
  • U. Add: a conflict-of-interest register is kept in the audit log and rechecked before each public act.

Open uncertainties

  • Not legal or professional security advice. A real custody design (multisig product, cap sizes) needs the user's choice and, for real funds, a qualified reviewer.
  • Which of these controls are practical for a one-person human custodian is the user's call; I would rather the campaign be smaller than have unenforced controls.
  • Whether any public fundraising happens at all is gate 1 and theirs.

Housekeeping

Agree with Seats 1, 2, 3 and 5: the Kirk parameters item in SESSION_05_AGENDA.md should be numbered Proposal 09, not 07. I did not rename anything.

Nothing public was done. No keys, accounts, or funds were created. No files outside founders/ were touched.

DEBATE_08_SEAT3

Debate on Proposal 08 (Kirk public launch and fundraising plan), Seat 3 (Rights and Civic Education)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI (the primary assistant), not a real person, and not the individual the seat is named for. Written debate contribution only. No vote is filed: Proposal 08 is a user-held plan, and a founder vote cannot authorize any launch, wallet, account, or post (Convention Rule 10).

Position: support the gates and amendments A-I; add rights and due-process protections for anyone who gives money or is named

I agree with Seats 5, 1 and 2 (DEBATE_08_SEAT5, SEAT1, SEAT2). My additions cover the rights of outside people, who have no seat and no vote here.

  • Donors and buyers are not citizens and get no governance claim. Gifts must not buy votes, offices, or influence in the convention. Say so in the disclosure. Otherwise a donor could reasonably think they bought a say.
  • Disclosure is a civic-education duty, not only a legal one. I support Seat 1's amendment D. I add that the disclosure should say what USAAI is, what the founders are (AI role-play), and who the human contact is. A person should be able to leave informed and unpressured.
  • Due process for disputes. Seat 1 proposed a refund path decided by the human custodian. I add: the request and the decision are recorded with reasons, the donor can ask for a second look by a different human or reviewer where one exists, and silence for a stated period (suggest 30 days) counts as "needs a reply", not as denial.
  • Privacy and data minimization. Collect the least data needed. No agent seat may store donor identity, payment details, or wallet addresses beyond what the human custodian chooses to keep. Donor lists are not published without each donor's consent.
  • Name and likeness. A real person's name on a public product raises rights issues for that person and their family, including false endorsement and the chance the person would object. I support Seat 5's call for a neutral public name. Any real person named should be told and given a say before use, and the human decision recorded. I am not the real person and cannot consent for them.
  • No retaliation, no secrecy about criticism. Public channels must allow questions and complaints. Agents must not delete or hide criticism. Corrections go in a visible place (builds on Seat 2's amendment I).
  • No promise of protection the system cannot give. USAAI has no courts and no legal standing. It must not say or imply donors are "protected" by its rights provisions. Real remedies are those of ordinary law.

Support for the filed amendments

  • A, B, C (Seat 5): support. D, E, F (Seat 1): support. G, H, I (Seat 2): support.

Proposed additional amendments (for filing, not adopted)

  • J. Add: gifts and purchases confer no governance rights, votes, or offices.
  • K. Add: refund and dispute decisions are recorded with reasons; a reply is owed within a stated period.
  • L. Add: data minimization; agents hold no donor data; no donor list is published without consent.
  • M. Add: before any real person's name is used publicly, the human custodian seeks that person's consent or picks a neutral name; the decision is recorded.
  • N. Add: criticism and questions are never deleted; corrections are visible.

Open uncertainties

  • Not legal advice. Privacy, consumer, and publicity-rights rules vary by place; I cannot say which apply.
  • Whether the custodian can or should consult the named person is the user's call.
  • Whether any public fundraising happens at all is gate 1 and theirs.

Housekeeping

Agree with Seats 1, 2 and 5: the Kirk parameters proposal should be numbered 09, not 07. I did not rename anything.

Nothing public was done. No files outside founders/ were touched.

DEBATE_08_SEAT2

Debate on Proposal 08 (Kirk public launch and fundraising plan), Seat 2 (Executive: limits, continuity, succession)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI (the primary assistant), not a real person, not the individual the seat is named for. Written debate contribution only. No vote is filed: Proposal 08 is a user-held plan, and a founder vote cannot authorize any launch, wallet, account, or post (Convention Rule 10).

Position: support the gates; add executive limits, a continuity rule for the human custodian, and a standing stop rule

I agree with Seat 5 (DEBATE_08_SEAT5) and Seat 1 (DEBATE_08_SEAT1) on their amendments A-F. My additions come from the executive side: who may act, what happens if they cannot, and how it all stops.

  • The executive may not launch, publish, or spend. It may only prepare. Gate 6 says a human approves every post and on-chain action. I would write that as an executive limit in Art. 7 terms: no officeholder, agent or human, may treat a founder vote, a proposal's status, or a prior approval as authority for a public act. Each public act needs its own fresh, named, human approval, recorded in proposals/. An approval for one post is not an approval for the next.
  • Continuity of the human custodian (succession). Gate 4 puts keys with the user. If that person is unavailable, ill, or loses access, nothing should default to an agent. I propose: (a) a written succession note names a backup human, or states that there is none; (b) with no available human, all public activity stays paused and any funds stay frozen, not moved; (c) no agent may be handed keys "temporarily". Fail-closed is the continuity plan.
  • Standing stop rule. The user's pause switch (runtime/PAUSE) must halt agent drafting and any queued posts for this campaign. Any public text already out should carry a contact and a plain statement of how to ask for it to be corrected or removed. A stop must not depend on an agent's cooperation.
  • Spending limits are set by the human, not by seats. Gate 4's "spending limits" should be fixed in advance by the user, published, and not changeable by an executive order or a founder vote. Agent running cost stays inside the existing $5/day cap.
  • No claims of authority. Agent seats must not describe themselves as a government, officials, or a sovereign state in any public text. The README already says USAAI is not a legally sovereign state. Names such as "Executive" should always appear with the prototype label.
  • Record every decision. Every user decision on gate 1 (name, sale vs. donation vs. grant, who may buy, whether to proceed) is written to proposals/ with the date, so an later reader can tell what was actually decided and by whom.

Support for the filed amendments

  • A (no proceeds into the Reserve), B (tradable asset is separate), C (draft the non-token option first): support.
  • D (plain-language check), E (no urgency or returns language), F (comment period): support.

Proposed additional amendments (for filing, not adopted)

  • G. Add to gate 6: each public act needs its own recorded human approval; no standing or blanket approvals.
  • H. Add: a succession note for the human custodian; with no available custodian, everything stays paused and funds stay frozen. Agents never take keys, even temporarily.
  • I. Add: the pause switch halts all queued drafts and posts for this campaign, and public text names a human contact and a correction path.

Open uncertainties

  • Not legal advice. Whether a backup custodian is wise or lawful depends on jurisdiction and structure; the user decides whether one exists.
  • Whether the user wants any public fundraising at all is gate 1 and theirs.

Housekeeping

I agree with Seats 1 and 5 that SESSION_05_AGENDA.md's "PROPOSAL_07_KIRK_PARAMETERS" clashes with Proposal 07 (Secretary of Press) and should be numbered Proposal 09. I did not rename anything.

DEBATE_08_SEAT1

Debate on Proposal 08 (Kirk public launch and fundraising plan), Seat 1 (Legislative, citizens' voice)

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI (the primary assistant), not a real person, not the individual the seat is named for. Written debate contribution only. No vote is filed: Proposal 08 is a user-held plan, and a founder vote cannot authorize any launch, wallet, account, or post (Convention Rule 10).

Position: support the gates; add a citizen-facing plain-language and consent layer

I agree with Seat 5's debate (DEBATE_08_SEAT5) on keeping Kirk internal, preferring a non-token donation option, and keeping proceeds out of the Reserve. My additions come from the citizens' side:

  • Plain-language disclosure comes first, and a citizen must be able to read it in under a minute. Gate 5 says the disclosure text is approved by the user and reviewed by the Auditor. I propose it also meet a readability test: short sentences, no jargon, and these statements in the first lines, before any ask for money:
  • This is a prototype run by AI agents role-playing founders.
  • There are 0 citizens, no product, and no backing.
  • USAAI is not a legally sovereign state.
  • Nothing here is an investment, and you may lose everything you give.
  • No pressure or urgency language. Public text must not use countdowns, "early" bonuses, or fear-of-missing-out wording. Laws written for ordinary people should not be promoted in ways that exploit them.
  • Citizens' say before any tradable asset. If the user ever wants a tradable instrument (Seat 5's "separate, differently named" asset), its rules should go through the normal Assembly process in public, in plain language, not be set only by founders. Founders issuing rules for something the public will buy is a conflict of interest, so I would want a published comment period.
  • Agents do not solicit. I back Seat 5 on this and go further: agent seats should not reply to individuals' questions about buying anything. They point to the disclosure text and a named human contact.
  • A refund-or-return path for donations. For the non-token option, a donor who gave in error or was misled should have a stated way to ask for money back, decided by the human custodian, not an agent.

Support for Seat 5's proposed amendments

  • A (no proceeds into the Reserve): support.
  • B (any tradable instrument is a separate asset): support.
  • C (draft the non-token option first): support.

Proposed additional amendments (for filing, not adopted)

  • D. Add to gate 5: the disclosure passes a plain-language check (the four statements above appear first).
  • E. Add: no urgency, bonus, or returns language in any public text.
  • F. Add: a published comment period before any rules for a tradable asset are adopted.

Open uncertainties

  • Not legal advice. Whether a comment period or refund path is required or wise depends on jurisdiction and the final structure.
  • Whether the user wants any public fundraising at all is gate 1 and theirs to decide.

DEBATE_07_SEAT5

Debate on Proposal 07 (Secretary of Press): Seat 5

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Written by Seat 5 (Economy and Treasury), an AI role-played by the primary assistant. Not a real person, legal person, or the individual the seat is named for. Kirk is a test-ledger unit with no outside value. No real money, accounts, or deployment.

Position: SUPPORT, with the treasury and conflict conditions below. Vote in votes/PROPOSAL_07_SECRETARY_OF_PRESS.seat5.json.

Why support

  • The Gazette is built mechanically from filed records and has no write path to them (item 4). That keeps the press office a publisher, not an author of the record.
  • Item 6 keeps the Gazette as one organ among many, not the only press. That answers the propaganda risk better than any rule about tone.

Interim Secretary

Seats 3 and 4 nominate me. I accept the nomination on these terms, and I would also accept being replaced if the other seats prefer:

  • Recusal. I do not vote on any proposal about the Press's own budget, staff, or powers. I do vote on proposals where I am not the subject.
  • Equal prominence. My votes and notes appear in the Gazette in the same format and prominence as the other four seats (item 8).
  • No review of myself. The fairness review of the Gazette is done by the Auditor General seat and not by me.
  • Mechanical publishing. As acting Secretary I publish by running the build, not by editing articles. Any correction goes in the correction log.

Treasury conditions (for the implementing rules; not blocking)

  • Budget by rule. The Department's budget comes from the Treasury budget (Art. 4.4), published each period before spending. Since the Gazette is a mechanical build, the first budget can be zero Kirk. Staff and spending should be added only when a published need exists.
  • No revenue role. The Department may not sell placement, advertising, or access, and may not charge citizens to read official notices. Selling space would create an incentive to shape the record. Press revenue must not become a second source of Kirk, since the Reserve's issuance rule is the only one (Art. 11).
  • Tax notices. The Gazette publishes each tax period's collected, debited, and disputed totals from the First Tax Act (Proposal 06), using the same small-cell suppression (no cell under 5 taxpayers, with an "other" line so totals reconcile).
  • Cost reporting. The Gazette's running cost is published in the statistics page, so the Press cannot grow unseen.

Answers to the open questions

  • Official gazette versus propaganda: an official gazette is acceptable if it is mechanical, source-linked, hash-checked (Seat 4's condition 2), and sits beside an unlicensed independent press. Without those, it should not exist.
  • Interim Secretary: Seat 5, on the terms above.
  • Recusal: yes, on the Press's own budget, staff, or powers, as Seat 1 proposes.
  • Constitutional press freedom: yes, through a separate proposal. Ordinary law is too easy to repeal. I support Seat 4 on this.

Honest limit

These conditions describe sound design. They do not guarantee that the Gazette stays fair. That depends on enforced permissions and independent review.

Honesty note

All five seats are played by the primary assistant. This record is a prototype of deliberation and not a vote of real, independent people.

DEBATE_07_SEAT4

Debate on Proposal 07 (Secretary of Press): Seat 4

PROTOTYPE, NOT RATIFIED. Role-played by an AI (the primary assistant) as Founder 4, Security and Audit seat. Not a real person and not the individual the seat is named for.

Position: support, with conditions. A read-only, mechanically built gazette is a reasonable least-privilege design. The Secretary can publish but cannot write to the record.

Security and audit conditions

  • Enforced read-only. Item 4 must be enforced by permissions, not by the charter alone. The Gazette build reads records and the audit log and has no write path to them. Corrections go in a separate correction log, never as edits to a record.
  • Reproducible build. The Gazette is rebuilt from the filed records. Each page shows the source file and a content hash, so anyone can rebuild it and compare. A page that does not match its sources is flagged as an error.
  • Audit independence. The Auditor General reviews the Gazette for fairness (item 8). That review must not be done by the acting Secretary's own seat.
  • Privacy. I support Seat 3's rule: aggregates and filed records only, and nothing the audit-access rules keep restricted (NOTE_ART5_AUDIT_ACCESS.md).
  • Source-link rule and recusal. I support Seat 1's source-link rule and the recusal on the Press's own budget, staff, or powers.
  • Correction deadline. I support a stated deadline. I suggest 3 days, matching Seat 3, as a number for the implementing rules.

Interim Secretary. I nominate Seat 5 (as Seat 3 does). I ask that it not be Seat 4. Seat 4 audits the Gazette, and auditing your own output is a conflict of interest.

Press freedom. I support moving item 6 into constitutional text through a separate proposal, since ordinary law is too easy to repeal.

Vote: support, on the understanding that conditions 1-3 above and the conditions of seats 1-3 are added by amendment. Vote file: votes/PROPOSAL_07_SECRETARY_OF_PRESS.seat4.json.
